\forall\omega\in\underline{\sigma_{1}}_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}},\omega\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{3}
and then
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{3}^{\star})}}(\omega)\not=\textit{\bf Y}
and then
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{3}^{\star})}}(\omega)=\textit{\bf N}
(here we use
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{3}^{\star})}}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}})\subseteq\{\textit{\bf Y},\textit{\bf N}\}
). As a consequence, using the homomorphic property of the measurement, we obtain
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma_{3},\sigma_{3}^{\star})}}(\sigma_{1})=\textit{\bf N}
, or in other words
\sigma_{1}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{3}^{\star}
. Analogously, we obtain
\sigma_{2}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{3}^{\star}
. As a result, we obtain
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2})\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{3}^{\star}
. But we have assumed
\sigma_{3}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2})
which leads to the announced contradiction. We have then proved the negation of the property (
2.116
).

Let us now imagine that the property (
2.115
) is not satisfied. Then, there exists a subset
U
of
\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
which is minimal for the inclusion among the subsets of
\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
satisfying
\sigma=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{\mathfrak{S}}}U
, and there exists
\sigma^{\prime}
element of
\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\smallsetminus U
.

Let us then introduce
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
such that
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\varnothing
and
U_{1}\cup U_{2}=U
. Now, we define
\sigma_{1}:=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{\mathfrak{S}}}U_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}:=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{\mathfrak{S}}}U_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}:=\sigma^{\prime}
. They satisfy (
2.116
) which is contradictory. Then, we have proved the property (
2.115
).

Reciprocally, let us suppose that the property (
2.115
) is satisfied. We have then for any
\sigma\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\}
the basic result
U_{\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}=U_{\sigma}\cup U_{\sigma^{\star}}
, which leads to the property
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\star})}}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}})\subseteq\{\textit{\bf Y},\textit{\bf N}\}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.49
.

(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
is deterministic iff
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}},\underline{\perp})
is completely reducible, i.e. iff

\displaystyle\forall A\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;A=A^{\underline{\perp}\underline{\perp}},

we have

\displaystyle A\cup A^{\underline{\perp}}=\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}},\;\;\;\;A\cap A^{\underline{\perp}}=\varnothing.

(2.117)

Here, the operator
(\cdot)^{\underline{\perp}}
designates the operator from
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}})
to itself which maps
A
to
\{\sigma\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\forall\sigma^{\prime}\in A,\sigma\underline{\perp}\sigma^{\prime}\}
.

Proof.

We note that, at the end of the proof of Lemma
2.48
, we have shown the following result

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)\;\textit{\rm deterministic}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\},\;{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\star})}}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}})\subseteq\{\textit{\bf Y},\textit{\bf N}\}

(2.118)

the announced result is then obtained.
∎

Lemma 2.50
.

We have also obtained the following characterization

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)\;\textit{\rm NOT deterministic}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\exists\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\},\exists\sigma_{3}\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;\;|\;\;

(2.119)

\displaystyle\sigma_{3}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2})\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\sigma_{3}\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{1}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\sigma_{3}\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\sigma_{1}\parallel_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2}.

Proof.

This result has already been proved along the proof of Lemma
2.48
.
∎

Theorem 2.51
.

Let us suppose that
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
is a simplex space of states and let
\star
be defined by

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}},

\displaystyle\sigma^{\star}:=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}_{\alpha\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\!\!\!\!\smallsetminus\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\alpha.

(2.120)

Then,
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
is a real structure of
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Conversely, let us consider
{\mathfrak{S}}
a space of states which admits a real structure
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
. Let us suppose that
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
is a simplex space of states. Then, we have necessarily
{\mathfrak{S}}=\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Proof.

As long as
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
is a simplex space of states, the star map
\star
defined on the real structure of
{\mathfrak{S}}
is necessarily given by the formula (
2.120
).

The first part of the theorem is trivial to check.

Let us now assume that the real structure of
{\mathfrak{S}}
is a simplex space of states and let us prove that
{\mathfrak{S}}=\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Let us then consider two elements
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
in
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
such that
\neg\;\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}
. Let us suppose that
\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
.

As long as
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
is a simplex, we must necessarily have
\sigma^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
. As a consequence, we have
\widehat{\sigma\,\sigma^{\star}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
which contradicts the condition
\neg\;\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\star}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
imposed for a real structure.

As a conclusion,
{\mathfrak{S}}\smallsetminus\overline{\mathfrak{S}}
is empty.
∎

Definition 2.52
.

Traditionally, an Inf semi-lattice
{\mathfrak{S}}
is said to be
distributive
iff

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\forall\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\mathfrak{S}}\;|\;\sigma\not=\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\;\;\;\;\;\;(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma_{2})\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\exists\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\in{\mathfrak{S}}\;|\;(\,\sigma_{1}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\;\;\;\sigma_{2}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\;\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\;\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\,).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(2.121)

Lemma 2.53
.

A simplex space of states is necessarily distributive as an Inf semi-lattice.

Proof.

Let us first assume that
{\mathfrak{S}}
is a simplex space of states.
Let us consider
\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\mathfrak{S}}
such that
\sigma\not=\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
and
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma_{2})\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma
. We have then
\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\subseteq(\underline{\sigma_{1}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\cup\underline{\sigma_{2}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}})
. If we define
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}:=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{\mathfrak{S}}}(\underline{\sigma_{1}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\cap\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}})
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{2}:=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{\mathfrak{S}}}(\underline{\sigma_{2}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\cap\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}})
we check immediately that
\sigma_{1}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\;\;\;\sigma_{2}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
and
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\prime}_{2}
. As a result,
{\mathfrak{S}}
is distributive.
∎

Let us now exhibit the two simplest examples of simplex spaces of states called respectively
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{2}\cong{\mathfrak{B}}
and
{\mathfrak{Z}}_{3}
(they are given with their star operation):

u
u^{\star}
\bot

u_{3}^{\star}
u_{2}^{\star}
u_{1}^{\star}
u_{2}
u_{1}
u_{3}
\bot

(2.122)

Although many type of indeterministic theories can be build, we fix a principle that will describe a rather general class of them. This choice is principally motivated by Lemma
2.55
and Lemma
6.17
.

Definition 2.54
.

The space of states
{\mathfrak{S}}
equipped with its real structure
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
is said to be
a completely indeterministic space of states
iff,

\displaystyle\forall\sigma,\lambda\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\sigma\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\lambda^{\star},

\displaystyle\exists\kappa\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\kappa\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\sigma^{\star}\;\textit{\rm and}\;\kappa\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\lambda^{\star}.

(2.123)

Lemma 2.55
.

If
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
is completely indeterministic then
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}},\underline{\perp})
is irreducible, i.e.

\displaystyle\nexists A\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;(\,A=A^{\underline{\perp}\underline{\perp}},\;\;\;\;\varnothing\varsubsetneq A\varsubsetneq\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}},\;\;\;\;A\cup A^{\underline{\perp}}=\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}},\;\;\;\;A\cap A^{\underline{\perp}}=\varnothing\,).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(2.124)

Here again, the operator
(\cdot)^{\underline{\perp}}
designates the operator from
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}})
to itself which maps
A
to
\{\sigma\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\forall\sigma^{\prime}\in A,\sigma\underline{\perp}\sigma^{\prime}\}
.

Proof.

Let us consider a completely indeterministic
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
. Let us suppose that
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}},\underline{\perp})
is reducible and let us exhibit a contradiction. Let us then consider
A\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
such that
A=A^{\underline{\perp}\underline{\perp}}
and
\varnothing\varsubsetneq A\varsubsetneq\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
and
A\cup A^{\underline{\perp}}=\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
and
A\cap A^{\underline{\perp}}=\varnothing
. Let us fix
\sigma
an element of
A
. For any
\omega
satisfying
\omega\not\!\!\underline{\perp}\sigma
we cannot have
\omega\in A^{\underline{\perp}}
and then, by assumption,
\omega\in A
. Let us now consider
\lambda\in A^{\underline{\perp}}
. As long as
\lambda\underline{\perp}\sigma
and
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
being completely indeterministic, there exists
\kappa\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
such that
\kappa\not\!\!\!\underline{\perp}\sigma
and
\kappa\not\!\!\!\underline{\perp}\lambda
. From
\kappa\not\!\!\!\underline{\perp}\sigma
we deduce that
\kappa\in A
. However, from
\kappa\not\!\!\!\underline{\perp}\lambda
we deduce that
\lambda\notin A^{\underline{\perp}}
which is contradictory. This concludes the proof of the assertion.
∎

Although the previous notion of indeterminism is quite appealing, we will introduce another notion called linearity which emphasizes the notion of ”superposition” of states in the notion of indeterminism.

Definition 2.56
.

The space of states
{\mathfrak{S}}
equipped with its real structure
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
is said to be
linear
iff,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\overline{\mathfrak{S}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2})\raisebox{5.59721pt}{\,\rotatebox{180.0}{$\sqsupseteq$}}\,_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},

\displaystyle\exists\sigma_{3}\in{\mathfrak{S}}\;|\;(\,\sigma_{3}\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{1}\;\textit{\rm and}\;\sigma_{3}\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2}\;\textit{\rm and}\;\sigma_{3}\raisebox{5.59721pt}{\,\rotatebox{180.0}{$\sqsubseteq$}}\,_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma_{2})\,).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(2.125)

A generic class of linear and completely indeterministic spaces of states called
{\mathfrak{Z}}^{\prime}_{N}
(with
N\geq 2
) is given as follows

\sigma_{1}
\cdots
\sigma_{N}
\sigma_{1}^{\star}
\cdots
\sigma_{N}^{\star}
\bot

(2.126)

A space of states of this class will be said to be a
one-dimensional indeterministic space of states
(this space of states will be eventually called ”quantum bit” space of states).

3.
Characterization of hidden states

In the present section, we intent to clarify the notion of real structure and in particular to explore the possibility to derive
{\mathfrak{S}}
as a ”completion” of its real structure
(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}},\star)
, i.e. to build hidden states in a suitable completion of the space of real states.

3.1.
Preliminary remarks

Let us introduce some notations.

We will denote by
{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
the set of chain complete lower subsets in
\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
. Explicitly,we have for any
{\mathfrak{F}}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}},

(3.1)

\displaystyle\forall C\subseteq_{Chain}{\mathfrak{F}},(\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}C)\in{\mathfrak{F}},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}},(\sigma\in{\mathfrak{F}}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\sigma\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime})\Rightarrow(\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{F}}).

(3.3)

{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
equipped with
\subseteq
is a poset, and equipped with
\cap
is an Inf semi-lattice.

Due to Zorn lemma and the chain completeness of any element
{\mathfrak{F}}
of
{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
, we have
Max({\mathfrak{F}})\not=\varnothing
.
We then define the map
\Omega
sending any element of
{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
to its set of maximal elements :

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\Omega&:&{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})&\longrightarrow&{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})\\
&&{\mathfrak{F}}&\mapsto&Max({\mathfrak{F}}).\end{array}

We define on
{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
a pre-order denoted
\sqsubseteq
by

\displaystyle\forall F,F^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}),

\displaystyle F\sqsubseteq F^{\prime}\;\;:\Leftrightarrow\;\;\forall f\in F,\exists f^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}\;|\;f\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}f^{\prime}.

(3.7)

Then
\Omega
satisfies

Lemma 3.1
.

\displaystyle\forall{\mathfrak{F}},{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\Omega({\mathfrak{F}})\sqsubseteq\Omega({\mathfrak{F}}^{\prime}).

(3.8)

Proof.

Trivial.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{{\mathfrak{S}}},

\displaystyle\{\,\sigma^{\prime}\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}\;|\;\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma\,\}\in{\mathcal{F}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}).

(3.9)

Proof.

The only non trivial part is the chain-completeness of
\{\,\sigma^{\prime}\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}\;|\;\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma\,\}
. It derives from the chain-completeness of
\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
which can be derived from the down-completeness of
\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
using the star operation.
∎

Definition 3.3
.

We will denote by
{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}
the subset of
{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
formed by the collections
J\in{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
which admit a common upper-bound in
{\mathfrak{S}}
. Due to the down-completeness of
{\mathfrak{S}}
, the elements of such a collection admit a supremum in
{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}
is a lower subset (i.e. it is downward closed) of
({\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}),\sqsubseteq)
containing every elements of the form
\{\sigma\}
for
\sigma\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
.

Proof.

Trivial.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

We have already introduced the following map

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}&:&{{\mathfrak{S}}}&\longrightarrow&{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}\\
&&\sigma&\mapsto&\Omega(\{\,\sigma^{\prime}\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}\;|\;\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma\,\}).\end{array}

Definition 3.6
.

We also introduce the following map

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}&:&{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}&\longrightarrow&{\mathfrak{S}}\\
&&J&\mapsto&\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J):=\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}}}J.\end{array}

Lemma 3.7
.

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{{\mathfrak{S}}},\exists J:=\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma)\in{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J)=\sigma.

(3.16)

Proof.

Let us consider
\sigma\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}
and let us introduce
\sigma^{\prime}:=\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\{\,\alpha\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}\;|\;\alpha\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma\,\}
. We note that for any
{\mathfrak{l}}\in\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{\mathfrak{S}}
, we have
\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{\mathfrak{l}}(\sigma)=\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{\mathfrak{l}}(\sigma^{\prime})
and then, due to the condition (
2.82
), we then conclude that
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

The pair
(\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}},\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}})
is a Galois connection, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}},\forall\sigma\in{{\mathfrak{S}}},

\displaystyle J\sqsubseteq\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma)\;\;\Leftrightarrow\;\;\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J)\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\sigma.

(3.17)

Definition 3.9
.

We will denote by
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
the closure associated to the previous Galois connection, i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lclcl}cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}&:&{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}&\longrightarrow&{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}\\
&&J&\mapsto&\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\circ\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J).\end{array}

We will denote by
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
the set of closed elements in
{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}
, i.e.
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}:=cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}({\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}})
.

Theorem 3.10
.

We have as usual

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}},

\displaystyle J\sqsubseteq cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J),

(3.21)

\displaystyle\forall J,J^{\prime}\in{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}},

\displaystyle J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J)\sqsubseteq cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J^{\prime}),

(3.22)

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}},

\displaystyle cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\circ cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J)=cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J).

(3.23)

Lemma 3.11
.

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}},\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in J,

\displaystyle\sigma\not=\sigma^{\prime}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\neg\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}.

(3.24)

Proof.

Let us consider
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\alpha)
with
\sigma\not=\sigma^{\prime}
. Let us suppose that
\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}
. We have then
(\sigma\sqcup_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime})\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
and also
(\sigma\sqcup_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime})\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\alpha
, and we have obviously
\sigma\sqsubset_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}(\sigma\sqcup_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime})
. These points lead to a contradiction as we have assumed that
\sigma
is a maximal element of
\{\,\alpha^{\prime}\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}\;|\;\alpha^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\alpha\,\}
.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}},\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in J,

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\not\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\star}.

(3.25)

Note that this condition is equivalent to the following one

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}},\nexists\omega\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\{\omega,\omega^{\star}\}\sqsubseteq J.

(3.26)

Proof.

Let us consider
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\alpha)
.
If we had
\sigma^{\prime}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\star}
,
\alpha
would be a common upper bound of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\star}
in
{{\mathfrak{S}}}
. This point would contradict the fact that
\neg\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\star}}{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}
by the definition of the
\star
map.

The analogous formulation (
3.26
) is derived from (
3.25
) using the order-reversing property of the star.
∎

Lemma 3.13
.

The pre-order
\sqsubseteq
defined on
{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})
is in fact a partial order on
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
.

Proof.

Let us consider
J
and
J^{\prime}
two elements of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
and let us assume that
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}\sqsubseteq J
. Due to
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}
, we deduce that for any
\sigma\in J
there exists
\sigma^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that
\sigma\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
. For this
\sigma^{\prime}
, the property
J^{\prime}\sqsubseteq J
implies that there exists
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in J
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime\prime}
. However, due to the property (
3.24
) we necessarily have
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime\prime}
, and then
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
by antisymmetry of
\sqsupseteq_{{}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.14
.

The infimum of two elements
J
and
J^{\prime}
of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
, denoted
J\sqcap_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J^{\prime}
is given as follows

\displaystyle J\sqcap_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Max\left((\downarrow_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}J)\cap(\downarrow_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}}}\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}J^{\prime})\cap{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\right)

(3.27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J\sqcap J^{\prime}

(3.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Max\{\,\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime}\;|\;\sigma\in J,\sigma^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\,\}.

(3.29)

Proof.

Let us consider
J
and
J^{\prime}
two elements of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
(i.e.
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J)=J
and
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J^{\prime})=J^{\prime}
). We first note that
(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq J
and
J
is in
{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}
, and then, using Lemma
3.4
we deduce that
(J\sqcap J^{\prime})
is in
{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}
.

Now, let us consider
J^{\prime\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
such that
J^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J
and
J^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J^{\prime}
. We have that, for any
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in J^{\prime\prime}
, there exists
\sigma\in J
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\sigma^{\prime}
, and then such that
\sigma^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}(\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\sigma^{\prime})
. As a result, we have
J^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq Max\{\,\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime}\;|\;\sigma\in J,\sigma^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\,\}
.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
I
and
I^{\prime}
be two elements of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
such that there exists
I^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
with
I\sqsubseteq I^{\prime\prime}
and
I^{\prime}\sqsubseteq I^{\prime\prime}
.

The supremum of
I
and
I^{\prime}
in
{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}
(denoted
I\sqcup_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}I^{\prime}
) exists and is simply given by

\displaystyle I\sqcup_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}I^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(I\cup I^{\prime}).

(3.30)

Proof.

Let
I
and
I^{\prime}
be two elements of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
. Because of the down-completeness of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
and of the fact that
I
and
I^{\prime}
admit a common-upper-bound, the supremum of
I
and
I^{\prime}
do exist.

The supremum
I\sqcup_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}I^{\prime}
must be the smallest closed element upper-bounding for
\sqsubseteq
the elements
I
and
I^{\prime}
. This supremum is then given by
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(I\cup I^{\prime})
. Note that the expression
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(I\cup I^{\prime})
is well defined because of the existence of
I^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Theorem 3.16
.

The map
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
is an isomorphism from
{\mathfrak{S}}
to
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
.

Proof.

In order to prove the injective property for
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
, it suffices to note that, for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}}
, the property
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma)=\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma^{\prime})
implies
\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\circ\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma)=\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\circ\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma^{\prime})
, i.e.
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
(see Lemma
3.7
).

The surjectivity property for
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
is a consequence of the injectivity of
\Lambda^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
on
{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
.

As a left component of a Galois connection, the map
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
preserves infima.
∎

Lemma 3.17
.

\{\,J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\;|\;Card(J)=1\,\}
is isomorphic to
\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
. In other words,

\displaystyle\{\,J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\;|\;Card(J)=1\,\}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}.

(3.31)

and for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\{\sigma\}\sqcap_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\{\sigma^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma^{\prime}\}.

(3.32)

Proof.

If
\sigma\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
we have obviously
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma)=\{\sigma\}
.
Conversely, Let us assume that
Card(\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma))=1
and let us suppose that
\sigma\in{{\mathfrak{S}}}\smallsetminus\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
. We denote
\sigma^{\prime}
the unique element of
\Theta^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(\sigma)
. We then have
\sigma\not=\sigma^{\prime}
. However, we observe immediately that, for any
\alpha\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
,
\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{{}_{(\alpha,\alpha^{\star})}}}(\sigma)=\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{{}_{(\alpha,\alpha^{\star})}}}(\sigma^{\prime})
. Then, using (
2.82
) we deduce
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
which contradicts the assumption. We then obtain
\sigma\in\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}
.

The second property is trivial to check.
∎

Lemma 3.18
.

We have necessarily, for any
I
subset of
\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}

\displaystyle I\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle I=Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\;|\;J\sqsubseteq I\;\textit{\rm and}\;\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\}.

(3.33)

Proof.

Let us consider
I\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
.

Let
J
be a subset of
{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
satisfying
J\sqsubseteq I
and
\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}
. We have then
J\in{\widehat{{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}})}}
(we can then apply
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
on
J
). We have then also
\{\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\}=cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J)\sqsubseteq cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(I)=I
. Hence,
Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\;|\;J\sqsubseteq I\;\textit{\rm and}\;\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\}\sqsubseteq I
.

On another part, for any
\sigma\in I
we have
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq I
and
\widehat{\;\sigma\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}
, and then
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\;|\;J\sqsubseteq I\;\textit{\rm and}\;\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\}
. As a result, we have then
I\sqsubseteq Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\;|\;J\sqsubseteq I\;\textit{\rm and}\;\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\}
.

As long as we have
\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\;|\;J\sqsubseteq I\;\textit{\rm and}\;\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\}
the property
\neg\widehat{\;\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}
, we can use the antisymmetry of
\sqsubseteq
and we have then
I=Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}J\;|\;J\sqsubseteq I\;\textit{\rm and}\;\widehat{\;J\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\}
.
∎

Let us then deduce from previous lemma a fundamental formula that will be useful in many parts of our construction.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let us denote by
U:=\Theta^{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}(\xi)\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}\;\;
for any
\xi\in{\mathfrak{S}}
. We then have

\displaystyle\forall\kappa\in{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}},\exists\varphi\in U

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\forall\chi\in U,\;(\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\chi)\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}(\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\varphi).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.34)

Proof.

Let us first remark that, if we denote
U:=\Theta^{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}(\xi)
, we have in Lemma
3.18
that
Max\{\,(\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}V)\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U,\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\,\}=U
.

Let us now fix
\kappa\in{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}
. Let us denote by
X_{U}
the subset
\{\,\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\omega\;|\;\omega\in U\,\}
. We note that
\widehat{\;X_{U}\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}
because
(\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\chi)\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\kappa
for any
\chi\in U
and we have then

\displaystyle\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}X_{U}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}

\displaystyle\kappa.

(3.35)

We also note that

\displaystyle\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}X_{U}=\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\{\,\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\chi\;|\;\chi\in U\,\}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle Max\{\,(\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}V)\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U,\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\,\}=U.\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.36)

In other words,

\displaystyle\exists\varphi\in U

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\{\,\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\chi\;|\;\chi\in U\,\}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\varphi.

(3.37)

From (
3.35
) and (
3.37
), we have then obtained

\displaystyle\exists\varphi\in U

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\{\,\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\chi\;|\;\chi\in U\,\}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}(\kappa\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}}}\varphi).

(3.38)

This concludes the proof.
∎

3.2.
Reconstruction of hidden states

We will now proceed in the inverse direction, and we will show that the set of hidden states can be reconstructed from the set of real states as soon as this set of real states satisfies some minimal requirements.

Definition 3.20
.

We will define
a real space of states
to be a quadruple
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\star)
such that

•

{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a down complete Inf semi-lattice with bottom element
\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}
. We require
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
to be generated by its maximal elements, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\;\;\sigma=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\;\;\textit{\rm where}\;\;\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}:=\{\,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\sigma^{\prime}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma\;\}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}:=Max({\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.39)

•

if we denote by
({\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\sqsubseteq)
the pre-ordered set defined by

\displaystyle\forall I,I^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle I\sqsubseteq I^{\prime}\;\;:\Leftrightarrow\;\;\forall\sigma\in I,\exists\sigma^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}\;|\;\sigma\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma^{\prime}

(3.40)

then
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
is a given sub-poset of
({\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\sqsubseteq)
satisfying

\displaystyle\forall V\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;V\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}).

(3.41)

•

\star
is a map from
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
to
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},

\displaystyle(\sigma^{\star})^{\star}=\sigma,

(3.42)

\displaystyle\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\sigma_{2}^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{1}^{\star},

(3.43)

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\{\sigma,\sigma^{\star}\}\not\sqsubseteq J.

(3.44)

•

cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
is a map defined from
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
to
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
and satisfying

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle cl^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(J)\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

(3.45)

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle J\sqsubseteq cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J),

(3.46)

\displaystyle\forall J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J)\sqsubseteq cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J^{\prime}),

(3.47)

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\circ cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J)=cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J),

(3.48)

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},

\displaystyle cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(\{\sigma\})=\{\sigma\},

(3.49)

\displaystyle\forall J\in cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})),\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in J,

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\sigma=\sigma^{\prime},

(3.50)

\displaystyle\forall J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\,J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;J^{\prime}\in cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}))\,)\;\Rightarrow\;J\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.51)

From here, we will denote by
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
the set
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}))
. The set
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
is naturally equipped with
\sqsubseteq
which is a partial order on
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
because of the condition (
3.50
).

We will adopt the following Inf semi-lattice structure on
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
:

\displaystyle\forall J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\;\;\;\;J\sqcap_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J\sqcap J^{\prime}

(3.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Max\{\,\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}}}}\sigma^{\prime}\;|\;\sigma\in J,\sigma^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}\,\}.\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.53)

Indeed, we first note that
(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
because
(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
and because of the property (
3.51
). We can then form the entity
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime})
. Then, we have (i)
(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq J,J^{\prime}
implies, using (
3.47
) and the fact that
J
and
J^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
,
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq J,J^{\prime}
and then
cl^{\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq J\sqcap J^{\prime}
, (ii) using (
3.46
) we have also
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime})\sqsupseteq(J\sqcap J^{\prime})
. Now, we conclude by observing that
\forall\sigma\in cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime}),\exists\kappa\in J\sqcap J^{\prime}
such that
\sigma\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\kappa
and for this
\kappa
there exists
\omega\in cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime})
such that
\kappa\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\omega
. But now we use (
3.50
), to deduce that
\sigma=\kappa=\omega
. In other words,
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\sqcap J^{\prime})=J\sqcap J^{\prime}
. Therefore,
J\sqcap_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}J^{\prime}
being the largest closed element lower-bounding for
\sqsubseteq
the subsets
J
and
J^{\prime}
, we then deduce the first equality.

The second equality has already been proved.

We note that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
can be injected as an Inf semi-lattice in
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
by the trivial map
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\;\mapsto\;\{\sigma\}\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
(because of requirement (
3.41
)). Explicitly, we have, for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\{\sigma\}\sqcap_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}\{\sigma^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma^{\prime}\}.

(3.54)

In the following, we will denote shortly by
\sigma
the element
\{\sigma\}
of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
if necessary.

The bottom element in
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
is simply given by

\displaystyle\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}=\bot_{{}_{{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}.

(3.55)

{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
being a down complete Inf semi-lattice, the supremum of a collection of elements of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
can be defined as long as they admit a common upper-bound.
More explicitly, let
J
and
J^{\prime}
be two elements of
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
such that
\exists J^{\prime\prime}\in{{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}
with
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
and
J^{\prime}\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
, then the supremum of
J
and
J^{\prime}
in
{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
(denoted
J\sqcup_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}J^{\prime}
) exists in
{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
.

We note that this supremum is simply given by :

\displaystyle J\sqcup_{{}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}J^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\cup J^{\prime}).

(3.56)

Indeed, we note first that, because
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
and
J^{\prime}\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
we have
(J\cup J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
and using
J^{\prime\prime}\in{{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}
and property (
3.51
) we deduce that
(J\cup J^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. We can then form the entity
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\cup J^{\prime})
. Now, we observe that, if
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
and
J^{\prime}\sqsubseteq J^{\prime\prime}
, then
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J\cup J^{\prime})\sqsubseteq cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(J^{\prime\prime})=J^{\prime\prime}
. This concludes the proof of the formula (
3.56
).

We then denote by
{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
the generalized effect space associated to
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
and defined according to Definition
2.4
. The Inf semi-lattice structure on
{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
is defined according to (
2.4
).

Let us also define
\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
to be the sub Inf semi-lattice of
{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
formed by the elements of the following set

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\{\,{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})}\;|\;\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\},\sigma^{\prime}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma^{\star}\,\}\cup\{{\mathfrak{l}}_{{}_{(\sigma,\centerdot)}}\;|\;\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}\;\}\cup\{{\mathfrak{l}}_{{}_{(\centerdot,\sigma)}}\;|\;\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}\;\}\cup\{\,{\mathfrak{l}}_{{}_{(\centerdot,\centerdot)}}\,\}\;\;\;\;\;

(3.57)

We note that, for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma^{\star}
, the effect
{\mathfrak{l}}_{{}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})}}
is well defined because of the property
\neg\;\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\star}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}
(see condition (
3.44
)).

Endly, we can define an evaluation map denoted
\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
as in (
2.5
).

The homomorphic properties of the evaluation map, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall\{\,{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}\;|\;i\in I\,\}\subseteq\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}},\forall J\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{\bigsqcap{}_{i\in I}^{{\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}}{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}(J)=\bigwedge{}_{\!\!i\in I}\;\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{i}}(J),

(3.58)

\displaystyle\forall{\mathfrak{l}}\in\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}},\forall\{\,J_{i}\;|\;i\in I\,\}\subseteq{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},

\displaystyle\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}_{i\in I}J_{i})=\bigwedge{}_{\!\!i\in I}\;\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(J_{i}),

(3.59)

are trivially true (this is a consequence of the construction of subsection
2.1
).

The property (
2.83
), i.e.

\displaystyle\forall{\mathfrak{l}},{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\,\forall S\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\;{\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(S)={\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}}(S)\,)\Leftrightarrow(\,{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathfrak{l}}^{\prime}\,),

(3.60)

is also trivially true (this is a consequence of the construction of subsection
2.1
).

We have to check now the property (
2.82
), i.e.

\displaystyle\forall J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;(\,\forall{\mathfrak{l}}\in\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}},\;{\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(J)={\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(J^{\prime})\,)\Leftrightarrow(\,J=J^{\prime}\,).

(3.61)

Let us fix
J,J^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
, we can exploit the property
\forall{\mathfrak{l}}\in\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}},\;{\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(J)={\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}}(J^{\prime})
by choosing successively all the effects
{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\star})}
for the different elements
\sigma\in J
. For any
\sigma\in J
we have
{\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\star})}}(J)=\textit{\bf Y}
and then
{\epsilon}^{{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}_{{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\star})}}(J^{\prime})=\textit{\bf Y}
, i.e. there exists
\sigma^{\prime}\in J^{\prime}
such that
\sigma\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\sigma^{\prime}
. As a result, we then obtain
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}
. By choosing now all the effects
{\mathfrak{l}}_{(\sigma,\sigma^{\star})}
for the different elements
\sigma\in J^{\prime}
, we obtain in the same way
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}
. As a conclusion,
J=J^{\prime}
(because
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
is a partially ordered set).

Endly, we observe that
\neg\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\star}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}}
because of condition (
3.44
).

We then conclude on the following theorem establishing the way to reconstruct the set of hidden states from the set of real states.

Theorem 3.21
.

We will assume that
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\star)
is a real space of states.

We define
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
to be the set
cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}))
. The Inf semi-lattice structure being given by (
3.53
). We define the generalized space of effects as in Definition
2.4
(it is necessary to use the formula for the suprema established in (
3.56
) ). The reduced space of effects is defined as in (
3.57
). Then, we define an evaluation map
{\epsilon}^{{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
on the whole
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
as in (
2.5
).

As a result,
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
is a well defined space of states which admits the real structure
({{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\star)
. The elements of
{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
are the real states, whereas the elements of

{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\smallsetminus{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
are the hidden states.

Let us now establish a result showing that there exists no hidden states in the completion
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
when
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a simplex.

Theorem 3.22
.

Let
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),cl^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\star)
be a real space of states. We will assume that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a simplex space of states. Then,
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let us begin by recalling that, as long as
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a simplex space of states, the star map
\star
is necessarily given by (
2.120
).

Let us consider two elements
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
such that
\neg\;\widehat{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
. Let us suppose that
\{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\}\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
.

As long as
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a simplex, we must necessarily have
\sigma^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\sigma^{\prime}
, i.e.
\{\sigma,\sigma^{\star}\}\sqsubseteq\{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\}
. As a consequence, we have
\{\sigma,\sigma^{\star}\}\in{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
which contradicts the condition (
3.44
).

As a conclusion, if
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a simplex space of states, then
{\mathfrak{J}}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\smallsetminus{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is empty.
∎

3.3.
Ontic completions

For the rest of this subsection, we will assume that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
satisfies the Finite Rank Condition (Definition
2.2
).

For the rest of the present subsection, we will consider
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\star)
such that

•

{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is a down complete Inf semi-lattice with bottom element
\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}
, such that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is generated by its maximal elements, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\;\;\sigma=\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\;\;\textit{\rm where}\;\;\underline{\sigma}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}:=\{\,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\sigma^{\prime}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma\;\}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}^{{}^{pure}}:=Max({\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.62)

•

\star
is a map from
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
to
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
satisfying

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},

\displaystyle(\sigma^{\star})^{\star}=\sigma,

(3.63)

\displaystyle\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\},

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\sigma_{2}^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{1}^{\star},

(3.64)

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},

\displaystyle\neg\;\widehat{\sigma^{\star}\sigma}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}.

(3.65)

We then intent to build the missing elements
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
and
cl^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
verifying the other requirements in Definition
3.20
.

Let us begin with our first basic definition.

Definition 3.23
.

We will consider a map from
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
to
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
denoted
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
and defined by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\,\}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.66)

(here, we are directly inspired by the Lemma
3.18
).

Lemma 3.24
.

{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
is a pre-closure. In other words,

\displaystyle\forall U\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle U\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U),

(3.67)

\displaystyle\forall U_{1},U_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle U_{1}\sqsubseteq U_{2}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{1})\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{2}).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.68)

Proof.

The property (
3.67
) is easily deduced from the definition (
3.66
). Indeed, we have, for any
\sigma\in U
,
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq U
and
\widehat{\;\{\sigma\}\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
and
(\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\{\sigma\})=\sigma
. As a result, we have
\sigma\in\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\,\}
and then
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)
. As a final conclusion, we obtain
U\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)
.

Concerning the property (
3.68
), we have the following analysis. For any
\sigma\in\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U_{1}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\,\}
there exists
V\sqsubseteq U_{1}
such that
\sigma=\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V
. But
U_{1}\sqsubseteq U_{2}
implies
V\sqsubseteq U_{2}
and then
\sigma\in\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U_{2}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\,\}
. As a consequence, we have
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{2})
, and then
\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V\;|\;V\sqsubseteq U_{1}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\,\}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{2})
. As a result, we obtain
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{1})\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{2})
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.25
.

We have then proved that
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
is
a pre-closure
. However, for some
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
it appears that
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
is not a closure. In other words,

\displaystyle\exists U\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)\not={\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U),

(3.69)

Let us consider the following case for the Inf semi-lattice
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
:

w
x
z
u_{1}
u_{3}
u_{2}
v_{1}
v_{2}
y
\bot

(3.70)

If we choose
U:=\{u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\}
we observe that
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)=\{w,z,y\}
, but we have
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)=\{w,z,y,x\}
.

Lemma 3.26
.

We have

\displaystyle\forall U\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle|

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)\sqsupseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U),

(3.71)

Proof.

Direct consequence of properties (
3.67
) and (
3.68
).
∎

Definition 3.27
.

We define the map
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
from
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
to
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
by

\displaystyle\forall U\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U):=\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(U)

(3.72)

(we have denoted
({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}
the n-th composition by
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
).

Lemma 3.28
.

cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall U\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle U\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U),

(3.73)

\displaystyle\forall U_{1},U_{2}\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle U_{1}\sqsubseteq U_{2}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{1})\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U_{2}).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.74)

Proof.

The properties (
3.73
) (
3.74
) are direct consequences of the properties (
3.67
) (
3.68
).
∎

Theorem 3.29
.

Let us now suppose that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
satisfies the Finite Rank Condition (Definition
2.2
).

Then,
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
satisfies the idempotency property

\displaystyle\forall U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\;

\displaystyle cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)=cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U).

(3.75)

and the following algebraicity property

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\forall U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\forall\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U),\;\exists\;\textit{\rm$V$ finite subset of ${\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}$ with $V\sqsubseteq U$ such that}\;\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(V).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.76)

Proof.

First of all, by choosing
U
to be a chain in
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
(explicitly
U:=\{\,u_{i}\;|\;i\geq 1\,\}
with
u_{i}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}u_{j}
for
i\leq j
) in (
2.21
), we note that the Finite Rank Condition ensures that the chain
U
is stabilizing, i.e.
\exists n\in\mathbb{N}
with
u_{n}=u_{k}
for any
k\geq n
(this means that
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
satisfies the
Ascending Chain Condition
).

Secondly we note that, if
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
satisfies the Finite Rank Condition, we then have the following
algebraicity
property for
{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
:

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\forall U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\forall\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U),\;\exists\;\textit{\rm$V$ finite subset of ${\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}$ with $V\sqsubseteq U$ such that}\;\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq{\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(V).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.77)

We now intent to deduce a similar property for
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
.

Let us consider
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
such that
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)
. Using the Ascending Chain Condition deduced from the Finite Rank Condition, and the fact that the sequence
({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(U)
forms a nest for
\sqsubseteq
, we deduce that there must exist an integer
n
such that
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(U)
. From (
3.77
), we then deduce that there exists
V
finite subset of
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
with
V\sqsubseteq U
such that
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(V)
. Therefore, we obtain
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(V)=cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(V)
. As a conclusion, we have obtained (
3.76
).

Let us now come back to the proof of property (
3.75
).

Let us consider
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
such that
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)
. Using the already proved algebraicity property (
3.76
), we deduce that there exists a finite subset of
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
denoted
V
such that
V\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)
and satisfying
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(V)
. Then, since
V
is finite, there must exist an integer
k
such that
V\sqsubseteq({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ k}(U)
. Therefore, we have
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(V)=\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(V)\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ k}(U))=\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ(n+k)}(U)\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(U)
. As a result, we have obtained
\{\sigma\}\sqsubseteq\bigsqcup{}_{n\geq 1}({\mathfrak{c}}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})^{\circ n}(U)=cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)
. As a final conclusion, we have obtained (
3.75
). We have then obtained the inequality

\displaystyle\forall U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\;

\displaystyle cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U).

(3.78)

The inequality

\displaystyle\forall U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\;

\displaystyle cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}\circ cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)\sqsupseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U).

(3.79)

is a direct consequence of properties (
3.73
) and (
3.74
).
∎

Remark 3.30
.

We are convinced that the Finite Rank Condition imposed as a prerequisite for the previous theorem could be alleged. This is not the purpose of the present paper to enter into this detail, and we let this point for a future study.

Let us now introduce our second basic definition.

Definition 3.31
.

We define the following subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\,U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}\;|\;\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in U,\;\sigma^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\sigma^{\prime}\,\}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.80)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\,U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}\;|\;cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\,\}\;\cup\;\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.81)

and

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{J}}^{c}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}:=cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})).

(3.82)

Lemma 3.32
.

{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
satisfies the property :

\displaystyle\forall K,K^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},\;\;\;\;(\,K\sqsubseteq K^{\prime}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\,)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle K\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.83)

Proof.

First of all, we recall the property :

\displaystyle\forall K,K^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},\;\;\;\;(\,K\sqsubseteq K^{\prime}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\,)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle K\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}).\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.84)

Indeed, let us consider
K,K^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
such that
K\sqsubseteq K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
.
For any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in K
there exists
\kappa,\kappa^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}
such that
\sigma\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\kappa
and
\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\kappa^{\prime}
. By assumption we have
\kappa^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\kappa^{\prime}
, and we then deduce
\sigma^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\sigma^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof of (
3.84
).

Now, let us consider
K,K^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
, such that
K\sqsubseteq K^{\prime}
and
K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. We have, using monotonicity of
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
, the property
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K)\sqsubseteq cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K^{\prime})
. And
K^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
means
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. Now, we use (
3.84
) to deduce that
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K)\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
, i.e.
K\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.33
.

\displaystyle\forall K\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\;

\displaystyle cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}).

(3.85)

Proof.

Let us consider
K\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. By definition, we have
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K)\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
and then, using the idempotency of
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
, we have
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K))=cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K)\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. In other words,
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(K)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 3.34
.

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\forall U\in cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})),\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in U,

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}.

(3.86)

Proof.

It suffices to prove the following simpler property :

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\forall U\in{\mathfrak{c}}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})),\forall\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in U,

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}.

(3.87)

Let us consider two elements
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{c}}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(U)
. There exists
V,V^{\prime}\sqsubseteq U
which are maximal for
\sqsubseteq
among subsets of
U
satisfying
\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
and
\widehat{\;V^{\prime}\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
and such that
\sigma=\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V
and
\sigma^{\prime}=\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}V^{\prime}
. If we had
\widehat{\;\sigma\sigma^{\prime}\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
, we would have
\widehat{\;V\cup V^{\prime}\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
. This point contradicts the assumption of maximality for
V
and
V^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.35
.

{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\widehat{\;U\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;U\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),

(3.88)

Proof.

Let us consider
U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
such that
\widehat{\;U\;}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}}
. In order to check the property (
3.88
), it suffices to note that
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(U)=\{\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}U\}
and to remark that any singleton associated to an element of
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is necessarily in
{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
.
∎

Lemma 3.36
.

{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
satisfies

\displaystyle\forall J\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),\forall\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\},

\displaystyle\{\sigma,\sigma^{\star}\}\not\sqsubseteq J,

(3.89)

In other words, we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}).

(3.90)

Proof.

Let us consider
J\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
and let us suppose that there exists
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
such that
\{\sigma,\sigma^{\star}\}\sqsubseteq J
. Then, let us introduce
J^{\prime}:=cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}(J)
. Due to the basic properties of the closure
cl_{c}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
, we know that
J^{\prime}
satisfies
J\sqsubseteq J^{\prime}
. Moreover, from
J\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
, we deduce that
J^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. Now, we use (
3.84
) to conclude that
J\in{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
. This result contradicts the existence of
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}
such that
\{\sigma,\sigma^{\star}\}\sqsubseteq J
. This concludes the proof of (
3.89
).
∎

Lemma 3.37
.

\displaystyle S\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\forall\alpha,\beta\in cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(S),\;\alpha^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\beta.

(3.91)

Proof.

Trivial rewriting of the definition of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
.
∎

Theorem 3.38
.

({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\star)
satisfies the axiomatic of a real space of states.

Proof.

This result summarizes the previous lemmas.
∎

Definition 3.39
.

The real space of states
{\mathfrak{J}}^{c}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}
(defined according to Definition
3.31
) is called
the maximal ontic completion
of
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\star)
.

In the following, we will denote
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}),cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}},\star)
shortly by
(\!({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\star)\!)_{c}
.

Definition 3.40
.

A subset
U
of
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
is said to be
admissible
iff
U
is an element of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})
.

Definition 3.41
.

We will say that
the ontic completion of
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime},\star)

is complete
iff we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\supseteq

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{K}}({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\{\,U\subseteq{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\bot_{{}_{\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}}}\}\;|\;\forall\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in U,\;\sigma^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\sigma^{\prime}\;\textit{\rm and}\;\sigma\not=\sigma^{\prime}\;\Rightarrow\;\neg\,\widehat{\sigma\,\sigma^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}\,\}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.92)

It seems
a priori
appealing to require the existence of a complete ontic completion. However, it appears (except in degenerate case) that the ontic completion of
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}},\star)
cannot be complete. The following theorem clarifies this point.

Theorem 3.42
.

We will assume the following basic property for
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}},

\displaystyle(\sigma\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma^{\prime})\raisebox{5.59721pt}{\,\rotatebox{180.0}{$\sqsupseteq$}}\,_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}.

(3.93)

Let us now consider two distinct pure states
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
such that
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})\not=\bot_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
and
\sigma_{1}^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}
, we have then
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\widehat{\mathcal{K}}({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})
but
cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\})\notin{\mathcal{K}}({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})
, i.e.
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\notin{\mathcal{Q}}_{c}({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})
.

As a conclusion, if
{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}
satisfies the assumed property (
3.93
) and if there exists
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
such that
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})\not=\bot_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
, then the ontic completion of
({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}},\star)
cannot be complete.

Proof.

Let us fix
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}
in
{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
such that
\sigma_{1}^{\star}\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}
.

First of all, we note that, as long as
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})\not=\bot_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}
we know that there exists
\sigma_{3}
in
{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}
such that
\sigma_{1}^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}
and
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}
. Indeed, if it was not the case we would have
\bot_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\not=(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}(\bigsqcap_{\sigma_{3}\in\underline{\sigma_{1}^{\star}}_{{}_{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})=\sigma_{1}^{\star}
and then
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})=\sigma_{1}^{\star}
which implies
\sigma_{1}\sqsupseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{1}^{\star}
which is false. As a conclusion,

\displaystyle\exists\sigma_{3}\in{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}{}^{{}^{pure}}\;|\;\sigma_{1}^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}\;\textit{\rm and}\;(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2})\not\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}.

(3.94)

We have obviously the same result if we replace
\sigma_{1}
by
\sigma_{2}
.

Let us now use the assumed property satisfied by
{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}
.

We have
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})\raisebox{5.59721pt}{\,\rotatebox{180.0}{$\sqsupseteq$}}\,_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3},\sigma_{1}
and
(\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})\raisebox{5.59721pt}{\,\rotatebox{180.0}{$\sqsupseteq$}}\,_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3},\sigma_{2}
. We have then to distinguish the following two options : (1)
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})\sqcup_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}(\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})=(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})=(\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})
, or (2)
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})\sqcup_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}(\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})=\sigma_{3}
. Let us now show that the first option is excluded. We have necessarily
\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}\sqsubset_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{1}
. Using
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})=(\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})
and
\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}\raisebox{5.59721pt}{\,\rotatebox{180.0}{$\sqsupseteq$}}\,_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{1}
we then deduce that
\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}
, and then
\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{2}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}
, which contradicts the assumption on
\sigma_{3}
.

We have then obtained the necessary condition :
(\sigma_{1}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})\sqcup_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}(\sigma_{2}\sqcap_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3})=\sigma_{3}
. As a consequence, we deduce

\displaystyle\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}\}

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle Max\{\,\bigsqcup{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}V\;|\;V\sqsubseteq\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\;\;\textit{\rm and}\;\;\widehat{\;V\;}{}^{{}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\,\}={\mathfrak{c}}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.95)

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq

\displaystyle cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\})

(3.96)

But now we recall that (
\sigma_{1}^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}
or
\sigma_{2}^{\star}\sqsubseteq_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}}\sigma_{3}
) to conclude that
cl_{c}^{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}}}(\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\})\notin{\mathcal{K}}({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})
. Noting that, by assumption, we have
\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}\in\widehat{\mathcal{K}}({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}})
we deduce that the ontic completion of
({{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}},\star)
cannot be complete.
∎

3.4.
Morphisms and ontic completions

Let us consider a morphism
f
from a real states space
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}_{A_{1}},\star)
to another real states space
({\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}_{A_{2}},\star)
. Both of these spaces of states are supposed to be such that the conditions required for the existence of their ontic completions are satisfied. We denote
{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{1}}:={\mathfrak{J}}^{c}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}_{A_{1}}}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{1}}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}_{A_{1}}
, and analogously
{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{2}}:={\mathfrak{J}}^{c}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}_{A_{2}}}}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{2}}={\mathfrak{S}}^{\prime}_{A_{2}}
.

We can define a morphism, denoted
F
here, from the states space
{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{1}}
to the states space
{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{2}}
by

\displaystyle\forall\xi\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{1}},

\displaystyle\Theta^{{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{2}}}}(F(\xi))=cl_{c}^{{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{2}}}}(\{f(\omega_{A_{1}})\;|\;\omega_{A_{1}}\in\Theta^{{\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{1}}}}(\xi)\;\})\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;

(3.97)

such that the restriction of
F
to
\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A_{1}}
is equal to
f
.

4.
First remarks on bipartite experiments

After presenting the requirements for the tensor product of real state spaces, we propose a solution for this tensor product and demonstrate that it is at least suitable for the deterministic case.

4.1.
Building principles for the description of bipartite experiments

During this subsection, we consider the two states/effects Chu Spaces
({\mathfrak{S}}_{A},{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{S}_{A}}},\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{S}_{A}}})
and
({\mathfrak{S}}_{B},{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{S}_{B}}},\epsilon^{{\mathfrak{S}_{B}}})
equipped respectively with their real structure denoted by
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{A},\star)
and
(\overline{\mathfrak{S}}_{B},\star)
. In order to shorten certain expressions, we will denote
{\mathfrak{E}}_{A}:=\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{S}_{A}}}
and
{\mathfrak{E}}_{B}:=\overline{\mathfrak{E}}_{{\mathfrak{S}_{B}}}
.

We now begin with a basic set of requirements addressed for the description of bipartite experiments. We will denote by
{\mathfrak{S}}_{AB}={\mathfrak{S}}_{A}\boxtimes{\mathfrak{S}}_{B}
the corresponding space of states.
4
4

4

Throughout this short axiomatic introduction, we adopt the inadequate notation
\boxtimes
for the tensor product in order to allow for different candidates for this tensor product. These different candidates will be denoted
\otimes
,
\widetilde{\otimes}
,…

First of all, we will assume that the set
{\mathfrak{S}}_{A}\boxtimes{\mathfrak{S}}_{B}
admits mixed bipartite states and a completely mixed state

\displaystyle\forall\{\,\sigma_{i,AB}\;|\;i\in I\,\}\subseteq{{\mathfrak{S}}_{AB}},\;\;\;\;\;\;\bigsqcap{}^{{}^{{\mathfrak{S}}_{AB}}}_{i\in I}\sigma_{i,AB}\;\;\textit{\rm exists in}\;\;{\mathfrak{S}}_{AB},\;\;\;\;\;\;

(4.1)

\displaystyle\exists\bot_{{}_{{{\mathfrak{S}}_{AB}}}}\;\textit{\rm bottom element of ${{\mathfrak{S}}_{AB}}$}.

(4.2)

We moreover assume that

{\mathfrak{S}}_{A}\boxtimes{\mathfrak{S}}_{B}
admits a real structure denoted
(\overline{{\mathfrak{S}}_{A}\boxtimes{\mathfrak{S}}_{B}},\star)
(see Definition
2.22
).

(4.3)

Secondly, for every pair of states
\sigma_{A}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{A}
and
\sigma_{B}\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{B}
